      "raw_content": "Food & Dining8 min read\n\n# Germany Food Guide: Must-Try Dishes, Prices, and Tips\n\nBest local dishes, street food, restaurant prices, dietary options, and food safety tips for Germany.\n\nBy Hello Travel Team\n\nThis article contains affiliate links. If you book through them, Hello may earn a commission at no additional cost to you. Our editorial recommendations are independent.[Learn more](/affiliate-disclosure).\n\n![Germany Food Guide: Must-Try Dishes, Prices, and Tips](https://images.unsplash.com/photo-1659413084271-ca1345764e15)\n\n## TL;DR: What to Eat in Germany and How Much It Costs\n\nGermany’s must-try food ranges from hearty schnitzel and sausages to modern vegan bowls, with typical meals costing €10–18 (about $11–20) in 2026 for a casual restaurant and €4–7 ($4–8) for popular street food. Germany’s food scene is safe, diverse, and surprisingly budget-friendly.\n\nIf you’re wondering what to eat in Germany, imagine a day that starts with fresh bakery pretzels, continues with currywurst from a street stand, and ends with schnitzel or käsespätzle in a cozy beer hall, all without breaking your budget. In 2023, Germany welcomed over 30 million international tourists, according to the German National Tourist Board, and a big reason people come back is the food culture: local, seasonal, and increasingly international.\n\nFor most travellers in 2026, a realistic daily food budget is around €30–50 ($33–55) per person if you mix bakery breakfasts, street food lunches, and sit-down dinners. Street food like döner kebab and currywurst stays cheap, while mid-range restaurants in cities like Berlin or Munich typically charge €14–22 ($15–24) for mains. To keep track of these costs, the Hello app’s AI receipt scanning can log your bakery tickets, kebab receipts, and bar tabs in seconds, even if they’re in German.\n\nThis Germany food guide will walk you through must-try dishes, street food vs restaurant prices, dietary options (including halal and vegan), and practical tips on food safety, tipping, and paying in euros so you can eat like a local with confidence.\n\n## Germany Must-Try Food: Classic Dishes You Shouldn’t Miss\n\nGermany’s must-try food is a mix of regional comfort dishes like schnitzel, bratwurst, sauerbraten, and käsespätzle, with mains in mid-range restaurants typically costing €14–22 ($15–24) in 2026 and generous portions often big enough to share.\n\nIf you’re building your Germany food bucket list, start with **schnitzel** (breaded, fried cutlet, often pork or veal) served with potatoes or salad, commonly priced around €15–20 at casual restaurants. **Bratwurst** (grilled sausage) and **currywurst** (sliced sausage with curry ketchup) are everywhere, from Christmas markets in Nuremberg to late-night stands in Berlin, often under €7 for a filling snack. Germany is also known for **sauerbraten** (marinated pot roast), especially in western regions, and **käsespätzle**, a rich cheese-and-onion pasta from Swabia that’s a must for vegetarians.\n\nDessert lovers should look for **Apfelstrudel**, Black Forest cake (Schwarzwälder Kirschtorte), and **Berliner** (jam-filled doughnuts). In Bavaria, classic beer halls pair hearty plates with local lagers; expect a main plus beer to run €20–28 ($22–30) in 2026. According to the German Federal Statistical Office, restaurant and hotel prices have risen roughly 15–20% since 2019, so today’s menus reflect that, but portions remain generous.\n\nTo keep your trip organized, save your restaurant receipts in the Hello app with AI-powered categorization. It automatically tags food vs drinks and converts euros to your home currency, so you can see at a glance how your schnitzel habit is affecting your travel budget.\n\n## Germany Street Food vs Restaurant Dining: Costs and Where to Eat\n\nGermany’s street food is one of the best-value ways to eat, with dishes like döner kebab and currywurst starting around €4–7 ($4–8), while sit-down restaurant meals typically range from €10–25 ($11–27) per main course in 2026 depending on location and style.\n\nAcross cities like Berlin, Hamburg, and Cologne, **street food** is both cheap and multicultural. A **döner kebab** (Turkish-German pita or flatbread stuffed with shaved meat and salad) usually costs around €4–6 and can easily be a full meal. **Currywurst with fries** runs about €5–7 from an Imbiss (snack stand), and a bakery lunch of a filled roll plus coffee is often under €6. According to Germany’s national statistics office, food away from home has seen moderate price increases, but street food stalls remain a budget lifeline for locals and travellers.\n\nSit-down restaurants cover a wide spectrum. Casual spots and beer halls in 2026 typically charge **€10–15** for simpler dishes like sausages with potato salad, while more elaborate mains or steak in major cities can reach **€20–28**. A small beer (0.3L) is often €3–5, and soft drinks around €3–4. Expect higher prices near tourist sights like Berlin’s Museum Island or Munich’s Marienplatz.\n\nHere’s a quick comparison of typical food costs:\n\n| Type of Meal | Typical Price (EUR) | Approx. USD 2026 | Comment |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Bakery breakfast combo | €4–7 | $4–8 | Coffee + pastry or sandwich |\n| Street food (kebab, wurst) | €4–7 | $4–8 | Filling single dish |\n| Casual restaurant lunch | €10–15 | $11–16 | Main dish only |\n| Beer hall dinner + drink | €18–28 | $20–30 | Main + beer or soft drink |\n| Mid-range 3-course meal | €30–45 | $33–50 | Per person, no wine |\n\nIf you’re travelling with friends, Hello’s expense splitting lets you snap the bill, assign each person’s share, and handle multiple currencies automatically—handy when you’re rotating who pays at street stalls vs restaurants.\n\n## Dietary Needs in Germany: Vegetarian, Vegan, Halal, and Gluten-Free\n\nGermany is very accommodating to dietary needs, with widespread vegetarian and vegan options, growing halal offerings in major cities, and clear allergen labelling that makes it easier for gluten-free travellers to eat safely and well in 2026.\n\nVegetarians and vegans will find Germany far more welcoming than its sausage reputation suggests. Berlin regularly ranks among Europe’s most vegan-friendly cities, with dozens of fully vegan restaurants and many mainstream places offering plant-based burgers, bowls, and **käsespätzle-style** dishes using vegan cheese alternatives. Supermarkets stock clearly labelled vegetarian and vegan products, and menus often mark items as “vegan” or “vegetarisch.” According to a 2023 survey cited in German media, roughly 10% of Germans identify as vegetarian or mostly plant-based, which drives menu diversity.\n\nHalal options are particularly strong in cities with large Turkish and Middle Eastern communities, such as Berlin, Frankfurt, and Cologne. **Döner shops**, Middle Eastern restaurants, and some burger chains openly advertise halal meat; when in doubt, ask “Ist das halal?” and staff will usually answer clearly. For strictly observant travellers, sticking to vegetarian dishes and fish in non-halal venues offers extra reassurance.\n\nGluten-free travellers benefit from EU allergen laws that require restaurants to flag common allergens, including gluten, on menus or in information sheets. Look for breads labelled “glutenfrei” in bakeries and supermarkets. Because specialty options can be pricier, logging your grocery and restaurant costs in the Hello app’s multi-currency budget tracker helps you see how much you’re spending to meet your dietary needs and adjust plans accordingly.\n\n## Food Safety, Tipping, and Paying for Meals in Germany\n\nFood in Germany is generally very safe, tap water is drinkable across the country, and tipping in restaurants usually involves rounding up the bill or adding 5–10% when you’re happy with the service.\n\nGermany has strict food safety regulations and routine inspections for restaurants, street food stands, and supermarkets, making foodborne illness relatively rare by European standards. Locals happily drink **tap water**, which is safe throughout the country unless explicitly labelled otherwise. Street food, from currywurst to Christmas market snacks, is also considered safe when you choose busy stalls with good turnover and freshly prepared dishes.\n\nWhen it comes to **tipping**, service is technically included, but it is customary to leave a small extra amount. In 2026, rounding up a €27 bill to €30 or adding 5–10% is standard in cafes and restaurants. You hand cash or your card to the server and say the total you want to pay, for example, “Make it thirty, please.” For small bakery or street food purchases, tipping is optional; locals may leave coins in a jar but often don’t.\n\nCard payments (especially debit and major credit cards) are widely accepted, but some smaller bakeries or Imbiss stands still prefer cash. ATMs are common, but watch foreign transaction fees from your bank. The Hello app’s bank statement import and automatic exchange rate conversion can show how much those small percentage charges add up over a trip, helping you decide when to pull out cash instead of tapping your card.\n\n## Staying Connected, Food Delivery, and Using the Hello App in Germany\n\nStaying connected in Germany makes it easier to discover great food, use delivery apps, and track your spending, and an eSIM from Hello gives you instant mobile data without hunting for a local SIM card on arrival.\n\nGermany’s cities have strong 4G and growing 5G coverage, which is ideal for using **food delivery apps** like Lieferando and Wolt, as well as Google Maps or local recommendation apps to find the best kebab stand or bakery nearby. According to the European Commission’s 2023 digital economy report, over 90% of German households have broadband access, and mobile data usage continues to grow, which means restaurants increasingly rely on QR-code menus and online reservations.\n\nWith [Hello eSIM for Germany](/esim/germany), you can buy and activate data before your trip, land in places like Berlin or Munich already connected, and immediately check reviews or translate menus. Plans start from 5GB with live pricing, so you only pay for what you need. If you’re visiting multiple European countries, Hello’s coverage in over 200 destinations makes it easier to keep one setup across your route.\n\nOnce you’re eating your way through Germany, the Hello app helps you **track food expenses** using AI receipt scanning (even for German-language receipts), voice expense entry, and multi-currency support. Travelling with friends? Expense splitting with automatic exchange rates means you can rotate who pays for dessert or beer and settle up later without spreadsheets. This makes it much easier to stay within a realistic food budget while still saying yes to that extra pretzel or dessert.\n\n## Common Questions: What to Eat in Germany, Budgets, and Practical Tips\n\nGermany’s food scene offers hearty classics, cheap street food, and modern vegan options, with typical daily food budgets around €30–50 ($33–55) per person in 2026 if you mix bakeries, street stands, and mid-range restaurants.\n\n**Q: What are the top Germany must-try foods for first-time visitors?** A: Start with schnitzel, bratwurst or currywurst, döner kebab, sauerbraten, and käsespätzle, plus desserts like Black Forest cake and Apfelstrudel. Regional specialities shine: pretzels and Weisswurst in Bavaria, fish dishes in Hamburg, and hearty stews in the Rhineland.\n\n**Q: How much should I budget for food per day in Germany?** A: For 2026, a reasonable range is **€30–50 per person per day** if you combine a bakery breakfast (€4–7), a street food lunch (€4–7), and a sit-down dinner (€15–25). If you opt for fine dining or cocktails, budgets can rise to €60–80.\n\n**Q: Is Germany street food safe to eat?** A: Yes, Germany’s food safety standards are high, and tap water is safe nationwide. Choose busy stalls with fresh turnover and avoid anything that has clearly been sitting out too long, especially in summer.\n\n**Q: Can I eat vegetarian, vegan, or halal in Germany?** A: Absolutely. Major cities like Berlin and [Germany](/germany) hubs have abundant vegetarian and vegan restaurants, and halal options are common in areas with large Turkish and Middle Eastern communities. When unsure, ask staff or choose plant-based dishes.\n\n**Q: How can I keep track of my food spending?** A: Use the Hello app’s AI receipt scanning and budget tracking to log each meal in euros and your home currency.       "content": "A metal plate of papri chaat on a marble counter with chai and a menu.\n\nMadam Chutney\n\n## Xaver's\n\nSiblings Jakob, Theresa, and Xaver Portenlänger were all under 30 in 2018 when they bought a pub and spruced it up into Xaver’s, updating both the food and the culture of the traditional Bavarian style. The trio, whose family has run the Alter Wirt hotel in the Bavarian countryside since 1919, built on their long history in local gastronomy to create an exciting menu and vibe. Surrounded by dark green walls and velvet upholstery, guests can tuck into dishes such as lamb shank with parsnips and charred leeks, as well as a number of vegetarian and vegan options.\n\n Rumfordstraße 35, 80469 München, Germany\n 01577 1490313\n Visit website\n\n## Mun Restaurant [...] ## La Bohème\n\nAt Michael Urban’s lavish steak restaurant, the fine cuts of meat are flambeed at your table. Part of a new-build complex in the north of Schwabing, the ground-floor setting has exposed brick walls, a black ceiling, and red leather booths. The bold decor and fiery showstoppers make for a special occasion rather than a quick dinner. To get an idea of the price, the weekend brunch buffet, featuring caviar and martinis, will set you back by almost 100 euros.\n\n Leopoldstraße 180, 80804 München, Germany\n 089 23762323\n Visit website\n\nAn airy restaurant dining room with an exposed brick wall printed with the words Vote Against Prohibition, with leather booths and bar seats. [...] ## Doctor Drooly\n\nSome vegan restaurants in Munich can feel like they’re ticking boxes on a generic menu rather than cooking creatively, but not Dr. Drooly. This laid-back pizza place in Isarvorstadt offers fun combinations of plant-based toppings and dough that is left to ferment for 54 hours. Jana and Salvatore Stacca opened the shop in 2020 not long before the COVID-19 pandemic, and they’ve relied on takeaway orders ever since. While there are a few seats available, most people prefer to eat their dinner off the premises, straight from one of the bright-pink cardboard boxes seen piled high behind the counter.\n\n Häberlstraße 7, 80337 München, Germany\n 089 51702709\n Visit website\n\n## Gasthaus Waltz",

      "raw_content": "[Get ourApp](https://apps.apple.com/app/apple-store/id6469005148?pt=541474&ct=Eater_Main_NavBar_&mt=8)\n\n* [Dining Out](/dining-out)\n* [At Home](/eater-at-home)\n* [Culture](/food-culture)\n* [Travel](/travel-restaurants)\n\n[App](https://apps.apple.com/app/apple-store/id6469005148?pt=541474&ct=Eater_Main_NavBar_&mt=8)\n\n[The homepage](/)\n\nNavigation Drawer\n\n* [Login](/auth/login?returnPath=/)\n\n[Subscribe to Kangtown](/subscribe-to-kangtown)\n\n![Diners seated at outdoor tables beneath shady trees.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934823/shutterstock_1153005203.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=16.630434782609%2C0%2C66.739130434783%2C100&w=2400)\n\n![Diners seated at outdoor tables beneath shady trees.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934823/shutterstock_1153005203.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0.054288816503799%2C100%2C99.891422366992&w=2400)\n\nOutdoor seating at Viktualienmarkt market.\n\n [Alexandre Rotenberg/Shutterstock](https://www.shutterstock.com/g/AlexandreRotenberg)\n\n* [Dining Out](/dining-out)\n\n# The 30 Best Munich Restaurants\n\nSchnitzel and fresh-tapped beer at a century-old pub, a Michelin-starred tasting menu at a stylish hotel, a salad gone viral at a young hot spot, a late-night stop for currywurst and chips, and more of Munich’s best meals\n\nby [Kate Mann](/authors/kate-mann)\n\nThanks to its international association with the [famous Oktoberfest celebration](/2018/10/8/17937104/oktoberfest-germany-munich-beer-festival-history-what-to-eat), Munich immediately conjures up images of raucous beer halls and traditional inns, where servers pass out plates laden with meat, dumplings, and gravy. The reputation is only reinforced by the city’s strong culinary overlaps with the rich foods of the nearby Alps and southern neighbor Austria.\n\nBut hearty local fare doesn’t represent the full picture of this large city. Like any European metropolis, Munich sees global trends trickle through its food scene, while international restaurants serve Japanese and Korean tasting menus, colorful Lebanese sandwiches, Indian street food specialties, and other dishes from around the world. A healthy slate of new openings keeps diners entertained without overwhelming, though the buzz around the best spots does necessitate reservations.\n\nStill, the classic pubs are a good place to start to get to know the city. At first glance, they all follow a similar format — with wood paneling, staff in Bavarian clothing, and drinks in tankards — but they actually vary greatly. There are those with a modern feel, those catering to a more upmarket crowd, and others that haven’t changed in decades. Whether in pubs or restaurants or even at market stalls, local chefs excel the most when looking inward, reinventing dishes ingrained in Munich’s identity, or working with the abundance of small producers found across the region. So raise a glass, or a full liter of beer, to this often unexpected city and all it has to offer.\n\n[**Kate Mann**](https://www.katemann.co.uk/) **is a freelance writer based in Munich covering food, The bold decor and fiery showstoppers make for a special occasion rather than a quick dinner. To get an idea of the price, the weekend brunch buffet, featuring caviar and martinis, will set you back by almost 100 euros.\n\n* [Leopoldstraße 180, 80804 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=La%20Boh%C3%A8me%2C%20Leopoldstra%C3%9Fe%20180%2C%2080804%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 23762323](tel:08923762323)\n* [Visit website](http://www.boheme-schwabing.de/)\n\n![An airy restaurant dining room with an exposed brick wall printed with the words Vote Against Prohibition, with leather booths and bar seats.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933298/La_Boheme.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0.012500000000003%2C0%2C99.975%2C100&w=2400)\n\n![An airy restaurant dining room with an exposed brick wall printed with the words Vote Against Prohibition, with leather booths and bar seats.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933298/La_Boheme.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0.012500000000003%2C0%2C99.975%2C100&w=2400)\n\nLa Bohème\n\n## Görreshof Wirtshaus\n\nServing hungry passersby since 1893, this traditional pub is one of the oldest in the city and a great spot to sample some local staples. Tuck into the roast pork with crispy cracklings; the sausage salad with red onion and gherkins; or the Münchner schnitzel with a horseradish and mustard crust. Even if you’re not hungry, it’s worth popping in for a refreshing beer; the staff regularly taps the Augustiner straight from a wooden barrel. Found in the student district of Maxvorstadt, this place manages to feel timeless in an ever-changing part of town.\n\n* [Görresstraße 38, 80798 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=G%C3%B6rreshof%20Wirtshaus%2C%20G%C3%B6rresstra%C3%9Fe%2038%2C%2080798%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 20209550](tel:08920209550)\n* [Visit website](https://www.goerreshof.de/)\n\n![A plate of meat topped with frizzled fried onions.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934694/Go_rreshof_Wirtshaus.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C3.3%2C100%2C93.4&w=2400)\n\n![A plate of meat topped with frizzled fried onions.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934694/Go_rreshof_Wirtshaus.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C3.3%2C100%2C93.4&w=2400)\n\nGörreshof Wirtshaus\n\n## Julius Brantner Brothandwerk\n\nFeatured on menus across Munich, young baker Julius Brantner is ingrained in the local restaurant scene thanks to his sterling sourdough. With a contemporary design and just a few products on display, his two bakeries look nothing like most others in the city. Head over for the organic rye bread with fermented apples, but arrive early if you want to try the daily specials; the cinnamon buns and almond croissants sell out especially quickly. Julius can often be spotted out back, kneading dough, folding pastry, or checking the ovens.\n\n* [Nordendstraße 23, 80801 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Julius%20Brantner%20Brothandwerk%2C%20Nordendstra%C3%9Fe%2023%2C%2080801%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 215280990](tel:089215280990)\n* [Visit website](http://www.julius-brantner.de/)\n\n![A baker holds a dark loaf sliced in half.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933292/Julius_Brantner_Brothandwerk.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C11.839343636711%2C100%2C76.321312726579&w=2400)\n\n![A baker holds a dark loaf sliced in half.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933292/Julius_Brantner_Brothandwerk.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C11.839343636711%2C100%2C76.321312726579&w=2400)\n\nKathrin Koschitzki\n\n## Broeding\n\nThis understated leader in the Munich dining scene has been in business for more than 30 years. Using top-quality produce sourced from local markets, farms, and producers, head chef Manuel Reheis and his team come up with a daily six-course menu. The cheese plate makes regular appearances, bearing a selection from award-winning Allgäu affineur Thomas Breckle. The wine list is the result of long-established relationships and new connections with skilled vintners, most of them from Austria.\n\n* [Schulstraße 9, 80634 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Broeding%2C%20Schulstra%C3%9Fe%209%2C%2080634%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 164238](tel:089164238)\n* [Visit website](http://www.broeding.de/)\n\n## Mamma Bao\n\nQueues outside restaurants aren’t all that common in the city, but you’ll often spot people gathered outside this eatery in Maxvorstadt. Mamma Bao doesn’t take reservations for its much-hyped bowls of biang biang noodles, which are hand-pulled by specially trained chefs from northern China. Opened by Munich-born Ying Xu in 2022, the restaurant feels sleek and sophisticated, but the vibe is relaxed. Try the top seller with beef, tomato, and egg.\n\n* [Augustenstraße 31, 80333 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Mamma%20Bao%2C%20Augustenstra%C3%9Fe%2031%2C%2080333%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [Visit website](https://mammabao.de/)\n\n## Wirtshaus Eder\n\nThis easygoing pub in the hip district of Westend stands out for its drink selection. Working with some of Munich’s best craft breweries — including Tilmans Biere and Hopfenhäcker Brauerei — manager Johann Eder offers an ever-changing selection on tap and in bottles. You’ll also find single-malt whiskey Slyrs, which is distilled in the Alpine foothills. You can pair your booze with some comforting grub, including veal goulash or fleischpflanzerl (pan-fried meatballs). If you turn up when it’s busy, you may end up at a shared table — an ideal opportunity to exchange notes on the different beers.\n\n* [Gollierstraße 83, 80339 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Wirtshaus%20Eder%2C%20Gollierstra%C3%9Fe%2083%2C%2080339%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 85634355](tel:08985634355)\n* [Visit website](https://www.ederwirt.de/)\n\n## Café Frischhut\n\nThis old-school cafe in the center of town can seem very touristy at times, but it’s also a lovable, enduring part of Munich’s furniture. It has churned out deep-fried pastries since 1973, when late hours made it a hangout for partygoers after a night out. Sitting side by side with people grabbing breakfast before an early shift at work, they would sip average coffee and sober up on schmalznudel, Bavaria’s version of a doughnut. Today, service starts later and the clientele has changed, but otherwise it feels much the same.\n\n* [Prälat-Zistl-Straße 8, 80331 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Caf%C3%A9%20Frischhut%2C%20Pr%C3%A4lat-Zistl-Stra%C3%9Fe%208%2C%2080331%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 26023156](tel:08926023156)\n* [Visit website](https://www.facebook.com/pages/Schmalznudel%20Caf%C3%A9%20Frischhut/276057742495191/)\n\n## L’Oca Bianca\n\nMunich’s first Venetian cicchetteria (small plates bar) is the go-to place for an aperitivo. Make a stop at this part-deli, part-bar for a glass of fizz and a choice of appetizing bites straight out of Italy. Bread and polenta slices come with various toppings, including sarde in saor (pickled sardines) or Gorgonzola cream. For something more substantial, come at lunchtime for filled focaccia or dishes such as burrata with blood orange, red cabbage, and pistachio.\n\n* [Alter Messepl. 6, 80339 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=L%E2%80%99Oca%20Bianca%2C%20Alter%20Messepl.%206%2C%2080339%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 92587937](tel:08992587937)\n* [Visit website](http://www.locabianca.de/)\n\n![A panini overflowing with prosciutto.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934527/L_Oca_Bianca.jpeg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0.012500000000003%2C0%2C99.975%2C100&w=2400)\n\n![A panini overflowing with prosciutto.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934527/L_Oca_Bianca.jpeg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0.012500000000003%2C0%2C99.975%2C100&w=2400)\n\nL’Oca Bianca\n\n## Marktpatisserie Lea Zapf\n\nLea Zapf runs a patisserie in Viktualienmarkt, one of Munich’s oldest food markets. In about 100 square feet, she makes a small selection of delicate cakes and pastries that she lines up neatly in the display case out front. Her signature Luftikus is a kind of choux bun stuffed with fillings like pistachio cream and lemon curd. One of more than 100 stalls, Zapf’s shop is easy to spot thanks to the black-and-white-striped canopy and inviting, cushion-laden ledge that runs along two sides.\n\n* [Viktualienmarkt Abt, III Stand 20/21, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Marktpatisserie%20Lea%20Zapf%2C%20Viktualienmarkt%20Abt%2C%20III%20Stand%2020%2F21%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 24217605](tel:08924217605)\n* [Visit website](http://www.leazapf.de/)\n\n![A pastry bun on a plate topped with a swoop of white cream.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933305/Lea_Zapf_Marktpatisserie.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0.006065016982042%2C100%2C99.987869966036&w=2400)\n\n![A pastry bun on a plate topped with a swoop of white cream.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933305/Lea_Zapf_Marktpatisserie.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0.006065016982042%2C100%2C99.987869966036&w=2400)\n\nAnnette Sandner\n\n## Caspar Plautz\n\nPutting the spotlight on simple spuds at their hugely popular market stall in Viktualienmarkt, Theo Lindinger and Dominik Klier rustle up baked potatoes with toppings that show off the region’s produce and reflect the time of year. (It feels like the unofficial start to the season when the duo releases the shop’s summer or winter menu.) This year’s highlights include the Caesars Sebald with fennel, Parmesan, capers, and flaky smoked fish from Lake Starnberg, as well as the gado-gado with coconut milk, pak choi, and green beans.\n\n* [Viktualienmarkt Abt III, Stand 38, 80331 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Caspar%20Plautz%2C%20Viktualienmarkt%20Abt%20III%2C%20Stand%2038%2C%2080331%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [0179 6632748](tel:01796632748)\n* [Visit website](http://www.casparplautz.de/)\n\n![A diner slices into a baked potato with toppings.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933281/Caspar_Plautz.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.666666666667%2C100%2C66.666666666667&w=2400)\n\n![A diner slices into a baked potato with toppings.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933281/Caspar_Plautz.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.666666666667%2C100%2C66.666666666667&w=2400)\n\nLiska Henglein\n\n## Madam Chutney\n\nDishing up delights from Delhi and beyond, this large Indian restaurant was once a small in-the-know spot on the northern edge of town. With an emphasis on street food, owner Prateek Reen wanted to present dishes that were hard to find in Munich. After moving to the inner city, the restaurant shifted focus toward more readily available curries, but the kitchen still uses Reen’s old family recipes combined with handmade spice mixes. The snacky options remain the stars, such as the pea and potato samosas and the papri chaat with yogurt, mint, and tamarind chutney.\n\n* [Frauenstraße 11, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Madam%20Chutney%2C%20Frauenstra%C3%9Fe%2011%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [0176 61948731](tel:017661948731)\n* [Visit website](http://www.madamchutney.com/)\n\n![A metal plate of papri chaat on a marble counter with chai and a menu.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933311/Madam_Chutney.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C19.723231758457%2C100%2C60.553536483086&w=2400)\n\n![A metal plate of papri chaat on a marble counter with chai and a menu.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933311/Madam_Chutney.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C19.723231758457%2C100%2C60.553536483086&w=2400)\n\nMadam Chutney\n\n## Xaver's\n\nSiblings Jakob, Theresa, and Xaver Portenlänger were all under 30 in 2018 when they bought a pub and spruced it up into Xaver’s, updating both the food and the culture of the traditional Bavarian style. The trio, whose family has run the Alter Wirt hotel in the Bavarian countryside since 1919, built on their long history in local gastronomy to create an exciting menu and vibe. Surrounded by dark green walls and velvet upholstery, guests can tuck into dishes such as lamb shank with parsnips and charred leeks, as well as a number of vegetarian and vegan options.\n\n* [Rumfordstraße 35, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Xaver's%2C%20Rumfordstra%C3%9Fe%2035%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [01577 1490313](tel:015771490313)\n* [Visit website](http://www.xaver-s.de/)\n\n## Mun Restaurant\n\nNamed after Korean head chef Mun Kim, this upscale Asian fusion restaurant in a Haidhausen basement is a must for sushi fans. Turning to professional cooking later in life, Kim trained under prominent Japanese chef Makoto Okuwa before honing his skills through his own ventures. Diners at Mun can choose from an omakase menu of delicate sushi, or they can sample a mix of Korean and Japanese cuisine in a tasting menu featuring both fish and meat dishes, such as rib-eye steak with kimchi and black rice. If you’d prefer a completely vegan option, just let the restaurant know 48 hours in advance.\n\n* [Innere Wiener Straße 18, 81667 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Mun%20Restaurant%2C%20Innere%20Wiener%20Stra%C3%9Fe%2018%2C%2081667%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 62809520](tel:08962809520)\n* [Visit website](https://munrestaurant.de/)\n\n![Slices of bright sashimi mounded in a deep dish.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933316/MUN.jpeg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0.066975785677485%2C100%2C99.866048428645&w=2400)\n\n![Slices of bright sashimi mounded in a deep dish.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933316/MUN.jpeg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0.066975785677485%2C100%2C99.866048428645&w=2400)\n\nDavid Koplin\n\n## Biergarten am Muffatwerk\n\nSummer doesn’t feel like summer in Munich without a crunchy radish salad or a portion of creamy obatzda (Camembert with butter and paprika) in a beer garden. While the quality of food can vary from beer garden to garden, this spot adjacent to the banks of the river Isar is consistently good, and the ingredients are organic. With “only” 400 seats, it also offers a more intimate setting than many other beer gardens. Order the bratwurst with sauerkraut or the trout with rosemary potatoes, and grab some of the preprepared snacky options, too.\n\n* [Zellstraße 4, 81667 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Biergarten%20am%20Muffatwerk%2C%20Zellstra%C3%9Fe%204%2C%2081667%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 45875073](tel:08945875073)\n* [Visit website](https://www.muffatwerk.de/de/gastronomie/biergarten)\n\n![Customers at standing tables beneath umbrellas and at an outdoor bar beneath a night sky.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933314/Muffatwerk_Beer_Garden_.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0%2C100%2C100&w=2400)\n\n![Customers at standing tables beneath umbrellas and at an outdoor bar beneath a night sky.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933314/Muffatwerk_Beer_Garden_.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0%2C100%2C100&w=2400)\n\nMuffatwerk Biergarten\n\n## Sorry Johnny Kaffeebar\n\nOozing cool, Sorry Johnny matches style with sustenance. People are willing to cross town for the cheese scones, French toast with ricotta and jam, or sourdough topped with beetroot, avocado, and hummus. The compact space is dominated by the coffee station, where barista Florian Vogt uses beans from local roaster Merchant & Friends to produce smooth flat whites, espresso tonics, and other creations, all listed in scribbles on the tiles behind the bar. Katrin Brüggemann keeps things running smoothly, and the eponymous Johnny (Johannes Rühl) is in charge of the kitchen.\n\n* [Preysingstraße 42, 81667 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Sorry%20Johnny%20Kaffeebar%2C%20Preysingstra%C3%9Fe%2042%2C%2081667%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 23713041](tel:08923713041)\n* [Visit website](https://www.instagram.com/sorry.johnny/)\n\n### More Maps\n\n1. [The Best Restaurants in Tuscaloosa, Home to the University of Alabama](/maps/best-restaurants-tuscaloosa-alabama-university-bama-ua)\n2. [The Best Restaurants Near Ohio State University](/maps/best-restaurants-ohio-state-university-columbus)\n3. [The Best Restaurants in Bloomington, Indiana, Home of the Hoosiers](/maps/the-best-restaurants-in-bloomington-indiana-home-of-the-hoosiers)\n\n## True & 12 Handmade Ice Cream\n\nTrue & 12 is the cream of the crop when it comes to Munich’s ice cream parlors. Owners Davina Goldammer Utz and Rony Utz painstakingly develop the 12 available flavors, which are all made from scratch using milk sourced from grass-fed cows. Rather than relying on bases or powders, the duo sticks to directly imported raw materials, such as hazelnuts and pistachios from Italy. Look out for the lavender with black currant swirl, made by infusing milk with lavender buds overnight.\n\n* [Rosenheimer Str. 14, 81669 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=True%20%26%2012%20Handmade%20Ice%20Cream%2C%20Rosenheimer%20Str.%2014%2C%2081669%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 89043323](tel:08989043323)\n* [Visit website](http://www.trueand12.com/)\n\n![Scoops of light green ice cream in small dishes, served on a metal platter with a bowl of pistachios.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933325/True___12.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=3.5843373493976%2C0%2C92.831325301205%2C100&w=2400)\n\n![Scoops of light green ice cream in small dishes, served on a metal platter with a bowl of pistachios.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933325/True___12.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=3.5843373493976%2C0%2C92.831325301205%2C100&w=2400)\n\nDavina Goldammer\n\n## Fink's Südtiroler Knödelküche\n\nThe knödel (bread-based dumplings) and South Tyrolean wine at Fink’s transport you straight to the mountains of northeastern Italy. Doused in melted butter and plenty of Parmesan (as they should be), the dumplings come in classic forms — made with beetroot, cheese, speck, or spinach — as well as more experimental variations such as chard and walnuts. The restaurant is part of a family business that includes similar outlets in Zurich and Bolzano, Italy.\n\n* [Klenzestraße 40, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Fink's%20S%C3%BCdtiroler%20Kn%C3%B6delk%C3%BCche%2C%20Klenzestra%C3%9Fe%2040%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 24206602](tel:08924206602)\n* [Visit website](https://www.finks.eu/munich)\n\n![Dumplings in various colors.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933288/Finks.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.952632350785%2C100%2C66.094735298431&w=2400)\n\n![Dumplings in various colors.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933288/Finks.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.952632350785%2C100%2C66.094735298431&w=2400)\n\nJulian Bueckers\n\n## Bergwolf\n\nYou know you’ve had a good evening if you end up chatting with friends and strangers at this legendary late-night food joint. There is a menu, but most people come in for the same thing: currywurst and chips. This no-frills snack from Berlin consists of slices of sausage covered in curry ketchup, and it tastes best when eaten from a paper plate with a mini wooden fork. Bergwolf is located right next to the subway station at Fraunhoferstraße, making it a convenient place to pass a weekend night if you’re waiting for the trains to start running again around 4 a.m.\n\n* [Fraunhoferstraße 17, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Bergwolf%2C%20Fraunhoferstra%C3%9Fe%2017%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 23259858](tel:08923259858)\n* [Visit website](https://www.facebook.com/LateNightCurryWurst/)\n\n## Bingo Bistro\n\nNot everywhere can get away with serving a full head of lettuce as a starter to share, but Bingo Bistro does so with style — earning it a bit of social media hype. Topped with chives and served with croutons and dressing on the side, the dish is one of around 10 at this hot spot in the cool Glockenbach district. The rest of the menu has a slight French influence combined with strong regional touches; local suppliers are listed on the back of the menu. When reserving a table, bear in mind that the earlier seating might provide better lighting for a salad photo shoot.\n\n* [Westermühlstraße 13, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Bingo%20Bistro%2C%20Westerm%C3%BChlstra%C3%9Fe%2013%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [Visit website](https://www.instagram.com/bingobistro/?hl=de)\n\n## Doctor Drooly\n\nSome vegan restaurants in Munich can feel like they’re ticking boxes on a generic menu rather than cooking creatively, but not Dr. Drooly. This laid-back pizza place in Isarvorstadt offers fun combinations of plant-based toppings and dough that is left to ferment for 54 hours. Jana and Salvatore Stacca opened the shop in 2020 not long before the COVID-19 pandemic, and they’ve relied on takeaway orders ever since. While there are a few seats available, most people prefer to eat their dinner off the premises, straight from one of the bright-pink cardboard boxes seen piled high behind the counter.\n\n* [Häberlstraße 7, 80337 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Doctor%20Drooly%2C%20H%C3%A4berlstra%C3%9Fe%207%2C%2080337%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 51702709](tel:08951702709)\n* [Visit website](https://doctordrooly.de/)\n\n## Gasthaus Waltz\n\nServing elevated Austrian dishes, this smart inn will soon have you hooked on the country’s cuisine. Not-to-miss dishes include the venison sausages, served with chanterelle mushrooms and cranberries, and the Steirisches backhendl (Styrian fried chicken) with potato and watercress salad. Opened in 2023, the Gasthaus is run by Markus Hirschler and Stefan Grabler, who first made a name for themselves at wine bar Grapes, which helps explain the huge selection of bottles at the inn. Inside, light wood combined with green chairs and statement lighting provide a rustic feeling without the slightest hint of kitsch.\n\n* [Ickstattstraße 13, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Gasthaus%20Waltz%2C%20Ickstattstra%C3%9Fe%2013%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 90429847](tel:08990429847)\n* [Visit website](https://www.waltz-gasthaus.de/)\n\n![A restaurant interior with bright wooden floors, tables, and an accent wall lined with bottles.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933290/Gasthaus_Waltz.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C15.794424491192%2C100%2C68.411151017616&w=2400)\n\n![A restaurant interior with bright wooden floors, tables, and an accent wall lined with bottles.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933290/Gasthaus_Waltz.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C15.794424491192%2C100%2C68.411151017616&w=2400)\n\nRocka Studio\n\n## Polka\n\nOn a cold day, this candlelit restaurant in the charming French Quarter feels like a cozy living room. When it’s warmer, outdoor seating spills out onto Pariser Platz. Proud promoters of local produce, the Polka team works to create a diverse mix of share plates. Recent options included cauliflower with turmeric, tahini, and feta and herring with cucumber, caper berries, and sour cream. The dessert menu is small but always includes a one-Euro chocolate truffle to sway those who normally skip straight to coffee.\n\n* [Pariser Str. 38, 81667 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Polka%2C%20Pariser%20Str.%2038%2C%2081667%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 89068391](tel:08989068391)\n* [Visit website](https://www.polka-polka.de/)\n\n![A dark wood bar with stools, in front of a two-tone cream and teal back bar.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933322/Polka.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C12.725694444444%2C100%2C74.548611111111&w=2400)\n\n![A dark wood bar with stools, in front of a two-tone cream and teal back bar.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933322/Polka.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C12.725694444444%2C100%2C74.548611111111&w=2400)\n\nMaiwolf Photography\n\n## Avin\n\nThis elegant bar in the Glockenbach district serves creative, tapas-style dishes and a large selection of wines from Germany and beyond. Any questions about the drinks should be directed to sommelier Lukas Stepper, who owns Avin together with his friend Alexander Glocker. Stepper can suggest a glass that goes best with your beef tartare with crostini and pickled egg yolk or your caramelized eggplant with bonito and miso. Spread across two levels, Avin has a minimalist yet cozy feel thanks to the use of clean lines and warm tones.\n\n* [Am Glockenbach 8, 80469 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Avin%2C%20Am%20Glockenbach%208%2C%2080469%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 28940791](tel:08928940791)\n* [Visit website](https://avin.restaurant/)\n\n![A glimpse of a bar, through a dining room and doorway.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933279/AVIN.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.666666666667%2C100%2C66.666666666667&w=2400)\n\n![A glimpse of a bar, through a dining room and doorway.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933279/AVIN.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.666666666667%2C100%2C66.666666666667&w=2400)\n\nRocka Studio\n\n## Café Faber\n\nHaving gained a loyal fan base as a pop-up breakfast spot, Café Faber quickly filled up with customers when it opened permanent doors earlier this year. Leon Hänseler uses his experience from third-wave coffee shops across Germany to command the espresso machine, while Hannah Gratzfeld prepares brunch and lunch dishes, such as beans with feta, wild herbs, and a poached egg. Dotted with potted plants and linoleum-covered tables, the new digs still attract a hipster crowd, but the space is a good option for anyone looking for a nourishing start to the day.\n\n* [Zeppelinstraße 5, 81541 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Caf%C3%A9%20Faber%2C%20Zeppelinstra%C3%9Fe%205%2C%2081541%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [Visit website](https://www.facebook.com/faberbreakfast)\n\n## Paulaner am Nockherberg\n\nThis modern-looking restaurant is connected to the Paulaner brewery, one of the biggest names in Munich’s beer scene. Centered on the shiny brass brew kettles behind the bar, the space is flooded with natural light streaming in from large windows. The food is beautifully presented, including the brotzeit platter full of meats, cheeses, and spreads, which is ideal for sharing. To drink? Paulaner, of course, including a number of seasonal specials. When the weather’s good, the beer garden is a lively spot for a sundowner.\n\n* [Hochstraße 77, 81541 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Paulaner%20am%20Nockherberg%2C%20Hochstra%C3%9Fe%2077%2C%2081541%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 4599130](tel:0894599130)\n* [Visit website](https://paulaner-nockherberg.com/)\n\n## Boazeria Giesing\n\nYou can lose track of the hours at a Munich boazn. These small, often dimly lit bars are some of the city’s best drinking spots, the types of places where it’s hard to resist making friends, especially with a charismatic landlord or lady. In 2022, three Giesing lads, Maximilian Heisler, Philipp Steenbock, and Richard Strobl, opened what they define as a mix of a boazn and an Italian birrieria. Boazeria (see what they did there?) offers spritzes, craft beer, and bruschetta, as well as a gentle introduction to a lesser-known side of Munich’s beer culture.\n\n* [Freibadstraße 1, 81543 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Boazeria%20Giesing%2C%20Freibadstra%C3%9Fe%201%2C%2081543%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [Visit website](https://www.boazeria.de/)\n\n## Gaststätte Großmarkthalle\n\nTo start the day in true Bavarian style, you’ll need to try a weißwurstfrühstück (white sausage breakfast): Traditionally served until noon, the dish consists of white sausages, sweet mustard, doughy pretzels, and a glass or two of wheat beer. Residents are divided on the best purveyor, but many suggest Gaststätte Großmarkthalle. This Munich institution, found among the buildings at the city’s large wholesale market, makes its own sausages on site and is usually packed to the rafters on weekends. Lederhosen optional.\n\n* [Kochelseestraße 13, 81371 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Gastst%C3%A4tte%20Gro%C3%9Fmarkthalle%2C%20Kochelseestra%C3%9Fe%2013%2C%2081371%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 764531](tel:089764531)\n* [Visit website](https://xn--gaststtte-grossmarkthalle-qec.de/)\n\n![An outdoor seating area with tables beneath umbrellas advertising Paulaner brewery and shady trees.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934489/gaststa_tte_grossmarkthalle.png?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C5.5666666666667%2C100%2C88.866666666667&w=2400)\n\n![An outdoor seating area with tables beneath umbrellas advertising Paulaner brewery and shady trees.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934489/gaststa_tte_grossmarkthalle.png?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C5.5666666666667%2C100%2C88.866666666667&w=2400)\n\nGaststätte Großmarkthalle\n\n## Beirutbeirut\n\nPink pickles and vibrant greens characterize the sandwiches and plates from this much-loved Lebanese cafe in Sendling. Combined with house-made hummus, baba ghanoush, or falafel, the fresh flavors here are hard to beat, although sister restaurant Manouche (located a few minutes away) comes pretty close with its light Lebanese pizzas. If you want to try food from both locations, the teams don’t mind if you bring items from one to the other.\n\n* [Lindenschmitstraße 18, 81371 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Beirutbeirut%2C%20Lindenschmitstra%C3%9Fe%2018%2C%2081371%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 54045869](tel:08954045869)\n* [Visit website](http://www.beirutbeirut.de/)\n\n![A bowl of falafel topped with sauce, surrounded by pickled vegetables and other dishes.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934504/Beirutbeirut.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0.039032006245115%2C100%2C99.92193598751&w=2400)\n\n![A bowl of falafel topped with sauce, surrounded by pickled vegetables and other dishes.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24934504/Beirutbeirut.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C0.039032006245115%2C100%2C99.92193598751&w=2400)\n\nBeirutbeirut\n\n## Der Dantler\n\nOffering fine dining with a casual edge, Giesing’s gastronomic gem is found pumping out a hip-hop soundtrack on an otherwise quiet street. The attentive waitstaff arrive at tables in sneakers and sweatshirts and bearing well-rehearsed details about every dish. The lunch menu is small but includes the best pastrami sandwiches in town, while the evening brings a set menu featuring European dishes with occasional Asian influences. Limited opening hours require some planning, especially if you want to visit the wine bar next door, which is open only on Thursdays.\n\n* [Werinherstraße 15, 81541 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Der%20Dantler%2C%20Werinherstra%C3%9Fe%2015%2C%2081541%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 39292689](tel:08939292689)\n* [Visit website](http://www.derdantler.de/)\n\n![An unseen hand dollops a cream onto an ornately composed vegetable dish.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933283/Der_Dantler.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.65%2C100%2C66.7&w=2400)\n\n![An unseen hand dollops a cream onto an ornately composed vegetable dish.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933283/Der_Dantler.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C16.65%2C100%2C66.7&w=2400)\n\nRocka Studio\n\n## Mural Farmhouse\n\nWhen not in the kitchen, young chef Rico Birndt, formerly of Pasture in New Zealand, can often be found tending to a vegetable garden on the roof of the WunderLocke hotel. The stylish new property, in the depths of Sendling, is home to the Michelin-starred restaurant Mural Farmhouse. Using hyperlocal ingredients and working with producers committed to natural cultivation and animal welfare, Birndt’s team creates various menus, including a 14-course fine dining experience. The restaurant is part of Team Mural, which has a few other spots in the city.\n\n* [Gmunder Str. 27, 81379 München, Germany](https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=Mural%20Farmhouse%2C%20Gmunder%20Str.%2027%2C%2081379%20M%C3%BCnchen%2C%20Germany)\n* [089 262089079](tel:089262089079)\n* [Visit website](https://muralfarmhouse.de/)\n\n![A charred onion dish.](https://platform.eater.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/chorus/uploads/chorus_asset/file/24933319/Mural_Farmhouse.jpg?quality=90&strip=all&crop=0%2C19.268292682927%2C100%2C61.463414634146&w=2400)\n\n![A
